THERE were two passengers who sat facing each other as the train rolled into the station. The aisle was full of suburbanites crowding towards the open doors, but these two kept their seats. They were in no hurry. Finally one of them spoke. "It won't do you any good to wait, mister,' she said. "I'm not goin to furget this umbrella and walk out and leave it. I'm a little lame. That's why I don't crowd out with the rest of 'em." -With the look of an injured man, the other pas- senger got up and made his way as rapidly as pos- sible to the door.

PENMANSHIP NOT REQUIRED.— A clerk was sitting at his desk one day signing some papers, and he was in such a hurry that he was not doing hip very best—in fact, he was writing a wretched hand. A man entered. He said nothing for a while, but just stood and watahed the clerk write. Finally he said, in a husky .tone: "By George, I wish I could write as.good as you do." The clerk did not reply, but just -kept on writing. In a minute or two the man remarked : "Great guns, if •I Gould just write as good as you do?" By this time the clerk was annoyed, and the stranger re- peated : "Well, if I could just write as good as you do !" Then die clerk turned suddenly in his chair, and said angrily: "Well, what would you do?" "I'd go to China and write. labels on tea boxes."

LADIES' LETTER..

BIT DEAR NELEIE,

There is quite a charming variety of spring jackets coming into fashion now. I have seen several very pretty ones. There is the open-fronted coat which is too adaptable to be overlooked, and each spring it reappears in some new and modish form, which strictly follows the fashion of the moment. Coats are so exceedingly short that they fulfil the requirements of La Mode and give an extra length of skirt, and the vary abbreviated basques also serve to show the sheath-like fitting on the hips. The dark or self-coloured coat is worn with the one-seam cheek skirt, an-4 the very light coat is contrasted by a black or dark skirt, and this has a smarter effect than where the cos- tume is entirely of one colour.

In addition to the cirr.ing eoltliliere are the,

spring bodices, and a. pa.rticnlarly effective one is a plaid with the fronts of a cross-over bodice quite loose, while the lining fits without a wrinkle; these are crossed and pinned in their place at the sides. The great point is. to see thzst the shoulders are properly fitted. If one does not feel one's shoulder eearns lying ansily and comfortably it is a sure sign that, a bodice has "gone wrong." The back seam should never be altered, except in very exceptional cases; the only possible alteration to be made is in the under•arm seams and shoulder scams. Bodices are more difficult to make when they are quite plain, but an amateur can easily grasp the mysteries of the cros-over bodice. 'Lie chetnisette which fills up the V-shapßi opening at the neck should be removable at will, so that the bodice can he made ready for evening wear at a moment's notice. A transparent lace ehemisette is (Wag wear for warmer weather.

For a best block skirt I cannot do better than advise you to have one of the charming new figured alpacas. The spotted or floral designs are really lovely. The whole-black dress with a vest of white is most useful, and the skirt can be worn with any number of blouses. Voile, which is not nun's veiling, but a very fine canvas, makes de- lightful spring gowns ; it is particularly suitable for the " fichn " or crossed bodice now so fashion- able. I saw a dress of royal blue voile which struck me as very dainty. It had thee folds at the hem of the skirt, three more folds on the top'of the slave, and the bodice crossed under a very,' narrow band of blue ribbon which formed the waist-band. The small V opening at the neck was filled in with orange velvet, and a handsome piece of real lace edged the folded velvet collar. In millinery the variety which has come forward recently is simply delightful. White tulle turban bats are veiled with black tulle, which gives a har- monious greyness very becoming to the complexion. Jet or spel and a black-and-white Paradise plume are the most appropriate trimmings. In other cases the tulle toque will be all black, with a rosette of out white, tulle in front, fastened with a buckle of dejad gold. This holds a large white PAradise plume, .which curio back gracefully. Ilkek hats have the: brims lined with gathered w}:ito chiffon, and are trimmed with uncurled white feathers ; Marabouts tipped with bits ef coloured plumes, shaded wings and weeping willow feathers are among the newest styles for hats. We ; have returned to poppy-red, and cornflowers will be much won. Every shade of coral is fashion- able and the colouring of the new hats is a feast to ' the eye,

There is 'an attempt being made to introduce long Atringi 4f tulle or lace with hats that recall 4the Empire PirlpE... They are trimmed under- neath the brim. Mel' crowns are being put timidly forward, but I .(iouht, thhir success, as they aro rarely becoming. Corelhwers have blown out to the ftizs of windmills, and two suffice to all but cover the side of a hat. it is to he hoped that flowers will soon regain their normal proportions, else they will become absurdities. There is a tendency to the ridiculous in the pictnre.?queriesg of nur present wearing apparel which we must guard against. Tooitas with the crown of pnuffed lisse strewn with jwels or sequins have entire brim or borders of flowers, and here the pansy, wallflower, the rose, and nasturtium flourish, as the blossoms admit of being closely massed, and are not too protuberant. Sometimes the flowers compose the crown, and folds of chiffon form the brim, but these are fashions for warmer weather, and must wait Lr at least a couple of months, unless going )tuth until winter has really departed. Your loving JENNIE.

EXAMINER Y, MARCH 30, 1899.

(Copyright.)

• It was disappoultmg to nave inane no captureti but there was plenty of satisfaction to be derived from this evening'sraid on a house which was supposed to be uninhabited except by ghosts, for the millionaire, the lord, and the detective would certainly have fared very ill if they had been left to the tender mercies of their enemies.

"I ftel terrifically hungry," said Lord Alfred, as soon as he was able to rise to his feet, after his bonds had been untied.

"So do I," added Mr. 'Raleigh: "I could do wonders with a loaf, a pound of beefsteak, and a bottle of stout just now.:

"Then if I ;ere Ton," remarked the officer who had led the attack, 4I would clear out of this, and get a good feed at once." _

- "NC; that won't do, either," interposed Mr. RiZzen-Quicke. -"We must stop here now until the Duke of llapsbnrg-Solltescin , and his friend the Prince come back for us, as. they said they would do." --e.

- This speech caused several. inquiries_ to be made, and the police inspectorwho had come down from Scotland Yard began to feel very much, ehilied•-t the prospect of whaeT4+ belicy4l,!to -birte:e6ming sensation in the detective world. -

'Everything connected with the series of kid- napping cases had been very exasperating to such • would-be elucidators of the mystery as hailed from headluarFers, and ;various.,". papers had 'made , uncomplimentary referfnce to tie incompetency of those whose duty it wal to guard the public safety. When a case has been attended ,by unusually • sta4ling kirelimstanc4es,•the unmasking of ordinary roguejs. is ample reAson ,for self-congratulation. But when the vogues chance to bp members of the highest ranks of society, there is promise of additional kudos and ecfat to the people who track them to their lair.

"You are right, Mr. Rizzen-Quicke," said Lord Alfred. "We are no further on with our task than we were this morning, and I, for one, mean to pursue it to the bitter end. But there is no reason why we should famish over it, so I vote that we send for some food, and fortify ourselves for the work-that still remains to be done." This suggestion was carried out. But care was exercised to be as unobtrusive as possible in executing the errand, and an impromptu meal was made in the drawinrooin, , with the inside shutters closed. This prevented all risk of there being anything to shew that there had been intruders in the house during the absence of the Duke and the Prince ;•and when the trio had satis- fied their hunger they went back to the room in which the raiders, after much searching about the house, had found -them. Then they placed them- selves eiactly where they had been left, and had their bonds re-tied, so that they looked as if they had never been, disturbed. •

' "Now, you must be sure to leave .no signs of your presence anywhere about," said Lad Alfred, anxiously looking round ,him to make sure that no oversight had been made in fhe room' itself. "Our intended removal is .supposed to take place at mid- night, but it may' occur either sooner or. later, and you must .do nothing that Will interfere with our plan for getting right at the heart of the mystery. As.wp have already explained to you, it. will ,be impossible to rely upon our behaviour after we are once more under the influence of the Duke. He can make us do exactly as he likes, and after we leave here with him and his accomplices we shall have to depend entirely upon you to see that no further harm befalls us. There can be no doubt that the three disappearances for which we are trying to account are due to the same agency with which we have to deal now, and to whatever place we are taken, we may feel sure that it will be the same place to which the others have been taken before us. The most difficult point to determine at present is whether our predecessors are alive or not."

Lord Alfred did not need 'to repeat his injunc7 tions to be c,autipus, for everybody concerned was fully•aw,are of the' importance of that night's work. It meant either failure or success, and the fact that Mr. Rizien-Quicke had promised an additionally, large reward was a good reason for being even more zealous in the cause of justice than ever. "Five thousand pounds amongst you if the mystery of my son's disappearance is cleared up, , and ten thousand pounds if he is restored to me

_ Such was the purport of Mr. Rizzen-Quicke's promise, and it might well cause a little pleasur- able excitement in the breasts of the six men before whom the prospect of sudden enrichment was thus dangled. The hours dragged very slowly by, for the two prime movers in the drama did not start for Hammersmith again as soon as they had expected to do, owing to a circumstance which was as exa,sperating as it was unforeseen. Up a secluded - by-street; about half a mile from the house in which he resided; the Duke of Hapsburg-Solltesein had an establishment of which his society acquaintances knew nothing. Here lived certain subordinates of his, who were as unprincipled as himself was, and who allowed no scruples of conscience to-stand between therg and tile work for which they received very handsome remuneration. Here, also, was a coach-house, which accommoz. dated a capacious brougham and a private hansom, upon neither of which .was there the slightest mark which could lead to identification by uninitiated persons. The hansom was the successor of the one which had come to grief when Mr. Rizzen-Quicke had been sitting in it, and in the stables were a couple of fine horses which were accustomed to being driven in either single or double harness. On this particular night, the brougham and both horses were ordered out ;but it was just on this particular night that one of the horses had had the perversity to become so ill that it was impos- sible for it to work. Had it only been nec,essary to remove one person from Hamlet House, the hansom might have been made to do duty, but as, exclusive of the driver, it was intended to trans- port five men from one place to another, it was clearly of very little use.

"We must get another horse from somewhere," said the Duke, gnawing his lip with veiation at the, delay. "That • will take ' scale time, Otto," was the Prince's .gloomy Comment. "And besides, it is a very risky pro.ceedi.iik,, for it is an odd time of the night to hire a horse, and it would •be still more odd to attempt to buy: one. You had far better have followed' toy advice this afternoon, and have done away with the necessity of this journey." ."You speak like a fool, Carlos! I believe in removing all trace of our work, and there would soon have been a hue and cry if dead bodies had been found yonder. The fault lay with your sister, through fixing on so outlandish a place. "You forget that she was taken a little by surprise, and naturally thought of the gloomy old building whioh had attracted our attention when, we were driving home from Richmond a few days earlier. But what are you. going to do about the. horses ? " "We will wait a little while longer, until we see if Stefan's recuedies are quite useless. Perhaps the horise may mend, after all." Stefan, the coachman, who understood horses as well as an ordinary doctor understands his patients, came 'up at this moment, to say that there .was a slight improvement, and this encouraged the Duke to believe that the improvement would continue. But this hope proved fallacious, and it was decided at the finish to proceed with one horse only. Happily for their peace of mind, Stefan was able to adapt the brougham to being drawn by one horse. But, before a start was eventually made, so much time had been lost that early morning was well advanced. . •

To all appearances, however, the delay had been unimportant, for. there was nothini, to arouse the suspicion that Hamlet House had been visited by anybody since they had left it on the previous afternoon, and the three "prisoners" were trans- ferred to the brougham with no more difficulty than had been anticipated. They walked quietly to it, in obedience to the Duke's order., and though his power made itself less strongly felt than it had done before, they were still conscious of being under an infernal influence which might yet cost them dear if their confederates failed to act up to their instructions. The pace was necessarily slower ,than it .wantil have been if both horses had been used; but the pursuers were thrown off the track, nevertheless. On turning the corner of a short street in South Kensington, the passengers had been hastily dragged out and pulled into a sheltered area, while the carriage pursued its slow course again, followed at a little distance by a vehicle containing some plain-- clothes policemen, who never suspected what had happened to the three helpless men, who, gagged, bound, and wilfully passive, had no idea that their escort had lost sight of them.

CHAPTER XXIV. A JEALOUS PULL".

It must have been a very potent drug which the Princess Delilah administered to Mr. Price, for he was krill in a itupefied sleep when she awoke, some- what earlier than usual. Perhaps she had not felt quite sure that his sleep would be so lengthy or so profound, and she could but be aware that it would be difacult to maintain the confidence which

he displayed when he _ ati3 coiled her home, ne would be sure to attribute his untoward sleepines; to soydething more pote4t than the simple flesh- wounds he, had received, and he would put two and twc together until he saw plainly that he had been, egregiowsly .befpoled by the bewitching siren tvhd had stolen his heart.

But the Princess had her own plans for pre- venting exposure, and she did not feel at all uneasy respecting possible revelations. Mr. Price would be duly disposed of, where he would not be in a position to tell tales out of school, and, thanks to the diplomacy of the previous evening, nobody had seen them together after he had left the Countess Sforza's alone. The Princess had followed him to make certain that, in the event of the two attendants of "Madame Celia" failing in the task that ,was allotted to them, he did not escape altog,ether with the valuable necklace which she coveted, and for which she did not think the sacrifice of a life too great a price to pay. The fact that she and her confidential serving- woman dared to retire for a few hours shewed how fatally they believed 'Mr. Price to be in their power. But there was no disposition to daily longer than they, believed to be prudent, and the Princess considered that she had much necessary work to do before the arrival of her brother and his friend, who were 'to breakfast with her, by, appointment, at nine o'clo'ck. She was as careful and deliberate over her toilet as though she were an innocent maiden, upon whom as yet no cloud of trouble or guilt had descended, and, in spite of her devilish nature, she looked as fair and sweet as an angel when she re-entered the rnnm in which she left her uncon- scious victim. She lifted his eyelids to satisfy herself that there was no danger of his reviving inopportunely, and then coolly proceeded to rifle his pockets. The necklace was her first prize, and she was none the less pleased because the stones which composed it wens 'ven more beautiful than she had supposed. "I can realise a large sum on these," she mused, as soon as the gems have been taken out of their setting. But I fancy it will hardly be safe to dis- pose of any more in London. I will run over to Amsterdam, unless Carlos will go for me. But no —there is nothing like nerve; and we had better all remain on the spot. for a while. There cannot be anything .to turn suspicion on us, although to- morrow London Will be.ringing with the news of another sensational disappearance; and I hear that one of the richest of all Indian Rajahs is coming to England next week. He would be a prize worth winning, and I will win him. Then I shall be rich enough; and so will Otto and Carlos. Otto must marry me then; and with nothing but love to think of, I shall be madly happy." In this way did this strange woman occupy her thoughts, the . while she possessed herself of Mr. Price's remaining valuables, which consisted of a splendid gold watch and chain, a gold cigarette case, a set of diamond studs, and a diamond ring, in addition to-a purse which only contained a few pounds, and which, denuded of these, was restored to its pocket again. There was apparently no awklyjard delicacy about this beautiful, innocent- looking woman, for she also examined a pocket-case, to see if perchance it was spliplied-with documents out of, which capital could be made. Of that kind of inquisitiveness which wants to know everything, without sense or ,motive, she was utterly devoid; so when she found that the case only contained letters, she put them back one by one unread until her own name, written in a feminine handwriting, caught her eye. "Now, who is writing about me to Mr. Price? " she asked herself, feeling for the first time appre- hensive of danger. Hesitating not a moment to satisfy herself on this score, she rapidly scanned the letter which had aroused her interest; and when she had finished reading it, she was a trans- formed being; whom a sudden shock had brought face to face with madness. Her eyes seemed as if they would start Out of her head, and she literally foamed with rage. .. f'Serahol" she shrieked in a•frenzied voice, which brought her :attendant running into the room in terror. "Sertino, read this letter, and tell me if I am mad or dreaming t" Serano obeyed this command, but her mistress could see by her face that what she had read was • not reassuring for her. "Is it true *that the Duke has actually proposed marriage to another woman ? " "I am afraid it is, madame, if this is his hand- writing." "It is his, sure enough. But let me read it again. Then I shall know what to do. If he has really played me false, may God have mercy on him, for I won't! He has always pretended that love was impossible to him, but that after we were all rich enough to retike to a less dangerous life he would marry. me. I believed that as soon as we were married, when it would no longer be necessary for me to play with other men's hearts, I could make him love me, after all; and if my dream is not realised there will be nothing left for me to livo. for."

"0 madame, I beg of 'you, do not - take it so hard! You surely will not do anything terrible " "No, it will not be very terrible, for it will a. 31 be over in a few minutes. But I will not be the only one to go; and if Carlos has connived at this, he shall share Otto's punishment." Clearly, the Princess was beside herself, and the causes of all this commotion were the following letters, one of which had been enclosed in the other:

"Dear Mr. Price, —I told you that the Duke of Hapsburg-Solltesein had asked me to marry him, and as I have no old and tried friends but yourself I feel thoroughly justified in asking your advice concerning hini, especially as you have always desired me to treat you as the brother you might have been but for Arthur's sad death. Well, then, I am afraid there is trouble brewing, for although I 4E31 at times that I hate the Duke, I seem as though I were, constrained to accept him—and he actually vows that I have ahead", done so.. Will you read the note he pressed into my hand to- night, and do your best to get me out of this fix, by helping me to get away from London without his being able to follow me? lam afraid of him, and want to get away at once, so would like you to come here the first thing to-morrow morning, to arrange matters for my immediate departure. Don't fail me, old friend.—Yours, as ever, ALVINA. "P.S.—I enclose the note from the Duke." "Dearest Alvfna,—You made me so happy by promising to my me! How can I ever thank you sufficiently, my beautiful darling? I know now •why you hesitated. You actually thought that I cared for the Princess Delilah. Unfortunately, she seems to have taken a mild fancy to me, as you yourself have noticed. But I have never given her any cause to believe that I could ever care for her in return. Indeed, I dislike her heartily, and when we are married we will live far away front women such as she, who are not fit to associate with my sweet love. I am bringing you a wonderful present in the morning. Until then, my dearest, au revoir. —Your devoted OTTO." There 4s no such thing in the world as a wise man. or a wise. woman. One's talents or achievements may be colossal, yet we all have a weak spot in our armour; and the weak spot in the case of both the Princess Delilah and the Duke of Hapsburg- Solltesein was love, which spelt ruin for both of them, as we shall soon see. "The restorative, Serano t " ex-claimed the Princess, and .she was immediately furnished with some liquid in a glass. This liquid was poured down Mr. Price's throat, and it revived him on the instant. • "Wake up, Mr. Price," he was adjured. "You have life to save, and you must do it at once!" The bewildered man was some time in compre- Rending what was wanted of him; but when his trAt.; at last became clear, even the Princess herself 41. e I • not more anxious to begin operations at once. "We must take the police with us," said the latter, whom jealousy had worked up into a perfect fever of madness, .which fully bore out Lord Alfred Vavasour-Pomeroy's opinion that she was actually predisposed to insanity. Now that this latent curse had burst into flame, she was reckless of con- sequences, 'and could but think of two things—she had been betrayed; and she must be avenged, no matter what befell anything or anybody. This is a dangerous mood for a woman of strong paseions to be in, as was soon self-evident to Mr. Price. "Come," said the Princess Delilah, "it is yet rlfy; and we shall catch the tiger in his den. We must take the police with us; for there is a grand sight prepared for them, and they shall soon gaze upon all that is left of John Mills, and of Teddy Quicke, and offSarauel Alosenthal, and of a few more foolish men whom a woman has beguiled into the hands of the jackal and the tiger." "WOMEN'S CHAT."

The dressing-room of the Princess of Wales at Sand- ringham is a" marvel of fanciful and clever arrangement, lull of sduvenits from all parts of Europe, India and the' Colonies. Among the Princess's treasures is a lovely little tabl made entirely of Dresden porcelain for which she has a great affection; The pillar of the table is tall and slender, and is ornamented with fruit, fl iwers, and figures in lull, relief. The top is painted with a landscape, and the work througiaoct .is of the most dainty and perfect description. The table stands upon three feet. The ground of the porcelain is quits light in tone, some rich touches of Royal blue being prominent in the deccration, while the pinks and yellows of the flowers, and the bright green of the leaves, make a very perfect whole. The Queen of Roumania has just completed a very beautiful gift for the Church of Curtea d'Argesch, the oldest in the kingdom. lc consists of a manuscript copy of the Evangelium, used in the Eastern Church, written and illuminated by herself on thirty sheets of parch- ment. Each sheet has a border of massive silver, dein— tily engraved, and all are bound up together in a golden hinge of curious workmanship. The Queen has heel busy on the work for more than siX years, and it is in- tended as a memorial of her little daughter, Princess Marie, who died in childho )d. • i A new law has recently been gromulgated in Russia, forbidding the use of corse's by girls and women. In that country people have to observe what the Govern- ment orders, without arguing the point, but in America it is different,and when it is seriously proposed to follow this example across the Atlantic it would he sate to I predict 'that it will give rise tie Confreleralille; difficulties. A Bill has been introduced in the Wisconsin State Legis= lature, having for its object the protection of the health of women by making a law to prohibit tight lacing. It is of course easy to bring in such a Bill, and perhaps get 'it passed, but it will be interesting to know how it is possible to give effect to its oroviskees. It was fugges- ted that the police should be provided with tape measures, with full power to make use of them in all doubtful cases. That no doubt would largely add to the gaieties of constabulary life, but we may we:l bqieve ' Mrs. Helen Barker, the President of the Women's Tem- perance Union, who says that trouble will begin when the police attempt to enforce such a law. The Queen of Portugal is a great enemy to tight- lacing. Sne has always preached to the women of her Court against this evil, and it is mainly owing to her efforts that tight-lacing in,Porttigal is ort the decrease. Her Majesty says, and rightly, that the gradually red- dening noae,the headache,and frequent peevish irritabil- ity, the laboroue respiration and inability either to assume on easy pneition, or to move about' with grace and freedom, plainly derionstrate that tight-lacing is fraught,with discomfort. As regards the numerous ail- ments to which the votaries of tight-lacing voluntarily subject the mselvee, she goes on to Fay that these are generally recognised, though the f. 11 extent of- injury to health, and danger to life, owing to serious physiological derangements,are known only to the unfortunate victims of the habit, and their medical attendants. The Queen recently photographed, with the aid of the Reiatgen says a tight-laced woman, and was then able to show the deformity caueed by undue pressure on the internal arrangethents of the body. Many gowns for evening wear are being built with raiders. The mode is a very becoming one to tall slight women, tut needless to say these of opposite build will do well to leave the fashion severely alone. For shoulder knot ei; and the baiternarabout has put in an appearance once more. It is 'expensive and easily spoiled, which makes it all the more desirable in the eyes of the woman fashienable, as there is less chance of the trimming be- coming too popular. Woolen crêpe de Chino is a delightful fabrio, and pro- mises to be extensively worn a little later on. Grenadine and barege tee will be well to the fore as the bright days appear, a couple of slips at least e bring supplied with each gown. This arrangement allows of variations in the toilette at comparatively small expense. With regard to the new satin cashmeres, these are exquisite. They have the soft sheen of silk, and drape softly and artistically. Most of the latest embroidered robes are on satin cashmere.

The newest ruflias for the neck are of net covered thickly with spots or tufts of cbenille. When pleated, the net foundation is almosk lost 514 ht of: The spots or tufts alone catch the eye, and the effect is quaint and bccnming to a degree. Small ends of the net finish the ruffle in front, and these are usually ornEmented with a frill of the same fabric.

A new method has lately been tried in America to put down the large hat. Threats and appeals having alike failed to imr rove matters it was deck's(' to bring the force of spiritual condemnation aeainst its often gigan- tic croportions. The Calvary Methodist Church is one of the most fashionable in New York, and consequently the nuisance was greater there than anywhere. So the Paster declared this form of headgear to be" ungodly and obstructive to the view " and every woman was requested to rerhove it when service began. But on the following Sunday only a few cu prits in a very large con- gregation complied with the request. A cloakroom has now been built, where all large hats are required, or ad- mittance is refused, and when the last accounts left,( the great majority of women in the congregation were un- decided whether to submit to this treatment, or to join the Episcepal Church cicse by. BAKED EGGS.—Remove the ends from a- French roll, cut the remainder into slices, an inch thick. Cut the centre of each slice ont, and fry the rings in boiling fat. Drain and place on paper for a moment, then arrange them on a butt:Te3 dial]. Break into each ring, an egg, dust with pepper, and bake for a few minutes till the whites are set.

PRINCESS DELILAH.

BY MRS. GEORGE CORBETT, author of "When the Sea Gives up its Dead," "The Prince Physician," "The Vindication of Olive Trevethick," "The Star of Yukon," "The Adventures of an Ugly Girl," "Deb o' Melly's," "A Fair Blockade Runner," &c. •

CHAPTER XXIII.

TIIE LAST VENTURE.

Polly Rizzen-Quicke was feeling so anxious ana unsettled that she preferred to remain in her own rbom, lest her mother shouldyerceive her uneasiness. She knetv that her faihet and her lover were pursuing a dangerous .quest, although they had. assured. her to the contrary, and when six o'clock arrived without their return she felt sure that some harm must have befallen them.

"I have a good mind to go and see Lady Mora," she thought. .-"She may think it odd for me to go and ask questions' *concerning the whereabouts of her brother, but with so many 'mysteries sur- rounding me I cannot afford to heed feeble con- ventional:scruples. I will take Alice with me; she may possibly prove useful." • By touching the buttoriAf her electric bell Polly had usually ensured the immediate appearance of her maid, but on this occasion it required two or three summonses beforeiehe„pre,sented hmelf, and she would have been sharply reprimanded by her impatient mistress but for the unwonted agitation she was displaying. Her face was red with crying, and at tight of Polly she burst into loud sobs, which evoked the dismayed inquiry: • "Whatever is the matter, Alice? Have you heard some more dreadful news ? "

"No, miss," was the hysterically-uttered reply. "I havn't heard any news—l only wish I had., "Try and explain yourself; perhaps I can help you.,, "Oh, no, miss, you can't. And even if you could, it wouldn't be right. I have been a bad girl, and I am only getting the punishment I deserve." Polly saw that it was not much use pressing the question until her maid's emotion had subsided; but the latter had evidently come with the inten- tion of volunteering a full explanation concerning herself, and she was not long in recovering suffi- cient calmness to proceed with her task. "I know you will hate me, miss," she said, ."fdr what I have done, but I seemed as if I couldn't help it, although I don't' want to pretend that I havn't been to blame. You remember the night of the burglary, when the Mistress's diamonds and emeralds were stolen ? " "Ceitainly I do." "Well; it wasn't a burglary at,n,lll" "But the jewels disappeared." . "I, know .that, miss; and if I had been in my, right senses, like I am now, they wouldn't have done."

"Yon.inean.that you could have given the alarm in time to prevent the burglars getting away with their booty? " "No, miss. I mean that there wasn't Any burglars except me, and the man that I gave the necklaces to was my sweetheart; at least, I thought he was, for he behaved As if he was awfully in love with me, and I met him three or four times. I swear to 'you, miss, that I never thought of stealing anything in my life. But when he asked me all sorts of questions about the mistress's jewels, I just told him everything he wanted to know; and when he ordered me to steal the neck- laces, give them to him, and maks everything look as if burglars had been, in the mistress's room, I felt as if I miiit ao it, and didn't even stop to consider whether it was right or wrong." Polly was naturally, very much astonished at this information, for she had no difficulty in com- prehending that the hypnotist must have eiercised his evil poker upon Alice,, as well as upon people in higher ranks,of life.' 'Without doubt he and the shani detectiv Were one and the same -individual, and, although it would not be easy to decide whether Alice's confession had been prompted by repentance for her personal complicity in the robbery, or by resentment at the subsequent non- appearance of her pretended lover, the fact remained that it would throw a great deal of light upon the strange events which had lately befallen the Rizzen-Quickes. "You must tell all this to the police," said Polly, .‘for it may be. ',useful in helping us to find my brother.' "Oh, miss, don't give me up to the police! shall go mad if they put me in prison.", "I don't. think you will be put into prison, because I, for one, believe -you When you say that you felt compelled to do as you were told. My father said the same tiiing, and if he could be hypnotised:into doing:whatever he was ordered, it is no wonder that you could. You must do every- thing you can to help us now, and you shall not be punished. I don't suppose we can recover the diamonds .and emeralds, but if ever the thieves try to sell that star-sapphire they will betray them- selves."

"I don't think they have got the star-sapphire, Miss Polly. I didn't give it .to them. It must have been stolen before the other things, and nobody was worse puzzled over it than I was; but I dursn't say a word about it, for fear of it being found out that I had taken the necklaces." This was the full burden of Alice's confession, which varied little by subsequent questioning, and although Polly wished to spare her mother's feelings as much as possible, she thought it best to confide everything to her 'at once. Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke had been unlike her usual self ever since Teddy's disappearance, and had played a very placid part in more recent events. But after hearing Alice's story, much that had been dark became suddenly clear to her, and her grief-stricken apathy gave place to an eager desire for action.

"I see it all now," she said, looking better than she had looked for many a long day, for excitement had banished the pallor from her cheeks, and her eyes shone with a newly-awakened lustre which boded no good to anyone who should be proved to ,havo injured her beloved and only son. "I see it all now," she continued. "The same fiendish man has been at work all through, and I know now who his accomplice is. It is the Princess Delilah, and it was my star-sapphire which she was wearing at the opera. Alice was induced to betray me, . and to give my necklanes to a stranger, although she is naturally an honest girl. My husband, my son, and Mr. Mills all acted under similar influence when they cashed largo cheques for gold, and if Alice did not remove the star-sapphire, Teddy must have been hypnotised into taking it with him when he left home. In that case he gave it to the Princess Delilah, and she shall be made to confess what has become of him, for she must have seen him since we did."

The deduction was logical enough, and its importance seemed so great and immediate that Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke at once despatched a message to Scotland Yard for a couple of detectives, with- out waiting for her husband's return. Knowing what she knew now, she shared her daughter's uneasiness concerning the result of this afternoon's expedition, and felt that not a moment was to be lost ere a watch was set on the Princess Delilah jid her friends. _ _

Many things pointed to the supposition that the fake of Hapsburg-Solltesein and Prince Carlos of Mitylene were her - most likely collaborators in crime, and the idea that the Duke himself was the übiquitous hypnotist was, by this time, more than a suspicion. We have thus Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke and her daughter convinced of the turpitude of the Princess Delilah, while Mr. Rizzen-Quicke and his com- panions were becoming acquainted with the fact that the Duke and the Prince were equally culpable. As the afternoon wore away the three captives began to feel the pangs of hunger, but no refreshment was offered them, and it was difficult to conjecture whether this neglect boded good or ill to the objects of it, who were left hour after hour to their own devices.

The man whom Lord Alfred had engaged as a "shadow" had, unfortunately, mot with an accident which incapacitated him from duty for the present, and was lying unconscious at the West London Hospital. Yet the aituation was not quite so hopeless as some people thought, for Mr. Raleigh had emulated Lord Alfred in acting with a certain amount of foresight. He had left word with a colleague at Scotland Yard that if he failed to report himself by six o'clock he was to be followed to Hamlet House, Hammersmith, by a rescuing contingent. of efficient constables out of uniform, and when Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's messenger arrived at Scotland Yard, Hamlet House was already being raided by the police. Unluckily for the cause of justice, there was nobody left in the house for them to capture, for the fellows who had been helpful in entrapping our friends had returned to town soon after their employers, who believed their present scheme to be as easy of execution and as safe as all their others had hitherto been,

WARRINGTON LEAGUE,

CATRO-STRRET V. PADGATE.—This match which should have taken place at the Cairo-street Rooms on Monday last, has, through some unaccountable reason, not taken place. From what we can gather we understand that the Padgate Secretary (Mr. H. Granger) desired to postpone the match, giving as his reason that be was not able to raise a team. it appears thcugh, that he forgot the most inanortant part of the postponing business, viz., the inform- ing of the Cairo-street secretary of his intention. Consequently the Cairo-street secretary (Mr. D. Plinston, jun.) not knowing anything of Mr. Granger's intention, got a very strong team to- gether to meet the Pedgate Club. In this, they were disappointed as no Padgate team turned up; Further, we uuderstand that the Cairo- street secretary has sent in an appeal to the League Committee, claiming the match. Should the com- mittee award Cairo-street the match, they will be the winners of the League Cup and Medals. LEAGUE TABLE. Pts.

' THE PEACE CRUSADE. 1

THE NATIONAL CONVENTION.

AN HISTORIC GATHERING.

BY W. T. STEAD.

Snow was falling outside. London looked its worst. The bad luck in the matter of weather which has pursued the Crusade in the metropolis from the first clung to it to the last. The cold and the snow, however, were kit without St. Martin's Town Hall, and in that building all was glowing warmth and enthusiasm. The Convention was a fitting close to the series of meetings in connection with the Crusade of Peace which have been held in the 'fine building at Charing Cross. There, early in the Loudon campaign, one of the more crowded and enthusiastic of the metropolitan meetings was held: There, at a later date, assembled that great gathering of Labour delegates which sent to their co-workers throughout Europe the striking appeal to lend their energies to the international move- ment promoted by the Autocrat of All the Russia% Last of all comes the National Convention, a meeting historic in its importance—an importance infinitely greater than that suggested by its numbe s. Never in the history of this country has there been such an assembly as that which gathered in St. Martin's Town Hall on March 21st. From time almost immemorial the people of England have been accustomed to assemble in their town's meeting, with the Mayor in the chair and the officers of the Corporation present. That is the oldest form of expressing public opinion which exists. No nearer approach to a plebiscite is possible in Great Britain. Yet never since .there 'first were municipalities have .so many great meetings been held to promote one object as have been called in connection'with the approval of the Czar's Rescript. In every case opposition has been invited, and in no single 'instance has any attempt to carry an amendment to the resolution supporting the action of the Czar met with any- thing but ignominious failure. From first to last .the meetings have been unanimous, synipathetic, and enthusiastic. Each gathering has appointed its delegates to the National Convention, which•repre- Bents the quintessence of the opinion of the country. Comparatively small in their numbers,the delegates spoke with the voice of a people. It was good to gaze from the platform upon the six hundred delegates. Strong, earnest faces were everywhere. The men and the women who looked towards the speakers were the cream of the whole country. In their own districts they- are the leaders of municipal, religious, social, and political life. Busy men with many duties, they had left all to hurry to the metropolis at their own expense, in order to give expression to the national conscience ea the subject of the Czar's Rescript. The vast majority were men and women past the meridian of life, who have won their hard way to the posi- tion of leaders. Yet their heartiness was undimmed, and they laughed, cheered, and applauded with the utmost enthusiasm. The attention given to every speaker was intensely close. The gathering was, in fact, heart and soul with the wishes of the Czar for peace. it tne meeting was striking, the platform was none the less so. In the chair was the Earl of Aberdeen, taking his first, active part in public affairs since his return front Canada, and in putting himself in thetfront on such an occasion well upholding the traditions of his family and of his own public career. On his left were Lady Frederick Cavendish, Sir Henry Bemrose, M.P., Captain Pine, M.P.' Archde icon Sinclair, Mr. Robert Cameron, M. P., and Sir Lewis Morris. To the right were Sir Wilfrid Lawson, ths veteran temperance advocate and upholder of peace, Mr. Herbert Whiteley, who came as a delegate from the great Lancashire constituency which he represents in Parliament, Mr. W. R. Cremer, who for so many years fought the battle of International Ai bitration almost single-handed in Pbrliament, and Mr. Fred Maddison, who stands for Labour. In almost every case these leaders of thought had a Character outside their own public positions as leaders of men. They came as the delegates of vast communities.

At first. the Convention required rousing. The Earl of Aberdeen was earnest, cultured, telling, but he spoke briefly, merely introducing the subject of the Corm-110°n. The earliest note of warmth and passion came from the Mayor of Wolverhampton, appointed delegate at the finest town's meeting held in the town which. he represents in the inemory of living men. He, moved the first resolu- tion in these terms: "That the memorials to the Emperor of Russia and the British Government, which have been so extensively signed in connection with the National Movement of the Crusade of Peace, in support of the objects of his Majesty's Bescript, be hereby approved by this onvention." A seconder for this was found in Sir Henry Bemrose, who .defeated Sir William Harcourt at Derby at the General Election. One was bound to remember, listening to his strong, heartfelt support of the motion, that, from the beginning of the Crusade to the end, all efforts to extrant one word of sympathy or even approval from Sir William Harcourt have proved unavailing. From Lady Frederick Cal endish came the first thrill which went through the meeting. Standing by the side of Lord Aberdeen, this lady, pathetic in her widow's weeds, spoke with a simple earnest- ness, directness, and humour which brought an immediate response from the meeting. When the resolution was put to the meeting, the hand of every delegate went up in its support, and a burst of applause confirmed the demonstration.

second work of the .Convention was even more important. The meeting was asked lo pass a resolution in the following words: "That the following persons be nominated for the presenta- tion of the memorials to her Majesty's Government and the Emperor of Russia: The Bight lion, the Earl of Aberdeen, the Right Rev. the Lord Bishop of London, the Right Rev. the Lord Bishop of Rochester, the Right Hon. Earl Grey, Sir Joseph Pease, Bart.' M.P., Sir James Kitson, M.P., the Right Hon. John. Morley, M.P., the Right Hon. Leonard H. Courtney, M.P., the Right Hon. Herbert Gladstone. M.P., the Right Hon. Loid Farrar, the Right Hon. Shaw-Lefevre,' S. Woods, E,(1,, M.P., the Right Hon. James Bryce, M.P., Thomas Burt, Esq. M.P., the Rev. Dr. Clifford, J. A. Bright, Esq., W. T. Stead, Esq., Councillor Martineau (Birmingham), W. Randal 'Cramer, Esq., John Battersby, Esq. (Glasgow), J. G. Holyoake; Esq., Lady Frederick Cavendish, and that the General Committee be entrusted with the necessary arrangements and the mode of presen- tation." Briefly and earnestly moved by the Arch- deacon eS London, the resolution was seconded by Air Herbert Whiteley, M.P. I spoke in support o. !he motion._ _

_ This second resolution having been carried a third, which was not on the programme, was intro- duced by Canon Rawnsley and named. It pled!zed the Conference to the opinion that the work which has been so gloriously begun should not cease. What we hope is that from the nucleus ot' the brief Crusade will spring a great central organisation working for Peace and human brotherhood, forming a centre round which the frequently warring Peace societies can gather in unity of purpose, Last of all came the vote of thanks to the chairman. With this broke up a meeting such as has never previously bven held in this country, I am writing this column in the brief interval between the Convention and the great meeting in the Qucen's Hall. Mr. John Morley, who as to he the principal speaker at this, is ill, but his place will be taken by Mr. Leonard Courtney, than whom no better substitute could be found. A word about the Crusade in Scotland must suffice. I have hurried back to the nwtropolis from the far North with • the echoes of the great meetings still ringing in my ears. scotchinnen .are not usually demonstrative, bet they have given their opinion of the Czar's Reseript with .no uncertain sound. At Aberdeen the meeting was kept going until eleven o'clock at night, and the audience scarcely dwindled. ..The whole nation is Ilithu•iastio for an arr.st of nrn,‘.*,,no"- ,

Mr. Gleave, a werelaouseman at Messrs Crosfield's works and also a sick visitor of the club, went to the defend- ant's house and paid him 13s. 41. sick pay, for which he got a receipt. On the evening of the let March Mr. Gleave again visited him and the defendant told him that the abscess had not stopped runnicg,and the doctor advised him not to stop it. There was no sign of the Idefendant having been at work, and as a matter of fact Mr. Gleave did not know he had been working. Mr. Gleave went again on the 3rd March and paid him a sum of 9s 8d sick pay, and that amount the club con- tended was taken under false pretences. The club did not insist upon a doctor's certificate being shown every. week, and when Mr. Gleave asked the defendant for it he said he would send it. By his actions and demean- our the defendant represented to Mr. Weave that he was not fit for work, and was not working, and was entitled to draw the money. It afterwards transpired that the defendant was working at the time, and had been work- ing at the Winwick Asylum from the 27th February. He had therefore received 9s 8d sick pay whilst drawing wages elsewhere. Mr. (Sleeve saw the defendant agein on the 10th March, and found him lying on the sofa. He ashed for the doctor's certificate which had been pro- mised, and the defendant said the doctor told him one certificate would do instead of two, and he was going t get another and send it on. As a matter of fact, the defendant had not been near the doctor, and had swindled the club of a sum of 93 Bd. The rules stat that any member leaving his employment by di 3missal or on his own account should forfeit all claims on the club, audit was therefore clear that by obtaining work ; elsewhere the defendant had forfeited all claims to sick pay. The club had given him every opportunity to make amends. Rule 11 provided that if any money were ob- tained by members through imposition the person should repay all money together with a fine of 53. He (Mr. Browne) wrote the defendant asking for the money amounting to £1 3s to be refunded together with the fine of 53 and costs. The deb did not wish to be hard, and if the defendant would now publicly admit that he had taken the money whilst he was working, and pay the money mentioned in his tltir. Browne's) letter, he would apply fer an adjournment, and if the money were paid time case would be with- drawn. The Defendant: I shall not pay anything, sir. Alderman Roberts: Do you deny the statements made? The Defendant: I don't deny them, but I wish to tell you something abut the club and how the manes, is stopped. Alderman Roberts : The question is, have you taken the money knowing that you are not entitled to it ? The Defendant; I have had 9s. Bd. which I was not entitled to. The Deputy Magistrates' Clerk : Do you admit taking this 9s. Bd. under false pretences ? Tne Defendant: Ido sir. The Deputy Magistrates' Clerk: The magistrates can only deal with the charge. The Defendant: The firm have no right to atop the club money out of the wages every week. They don't give us any receipt and stop fourpence every week and twopence a month for the Infirmary. The Chairman : We cannot enter into that reader the present charge. The Deputy Magistrates' Cerk : You will have to ge to Quarter Sessions unless you comply with the arrange- ment. The Defendant: I am quite prepared to have the cis heard at Liverpool. The Chairman: I think you are very foelish. Mr. Browne: You cannot do other than commit him to the Liverpool Sessions unless be pleads guilty to ob- taining the money by means of a trick. The Deputy Magistrates Clerk: Do you admit ob- taining the money by means of a trick? The Defendant: No. Mr. Brovrne : Tt e case will cost the county £l4 or £l5. (To the defendant) : I will give you one more opportunity to accept my offer? The Defendant: No sir. The Chairman (to the defendsnt): We are quite willing to do anything to bring about a reasouable settlement. It seems to me that the club has made a fair offer. Any grievance you may have does not enter into the merits of the case. It may be true what you contend. But the case against you is that you have obtained this money under false pretences and you admit that. That is all we have to deal with. The offer is a very fair one, and one you ought to accept. The Defendant : I would like it to go to Quarter Sessions. The Chairman: Have you any friends in court to talk the matter over with. The Defendant: No sir. The Court decided that Mr. Taylor the Police Court Missionary should have a conversation with the defendant. On his return into Court, Mr. Taylor said he had been unable to get tbe defendant to change his opinion. Mr. Browne: The officials of the club are willing to apply for an adjournment if the defendant will pay the 9s. Bd. back. The Chairman (to defendant) ; Will you do that? The defendant: No sir. The Chairman : Well the case must proceed. Mr. Browne (to defendant) : Do you really want to go to Liverpool Quarter Sessions ? The Defendant: I do. James Gleave, warehouseman at Messrs. Crosfield's was the first witness called,and he bore out Mr Browne's statement. The defendant did not hand the club a docter's certificate from the 21st February when he was certified to Se ill until March 9th when he was declared fit for work. On the Bth March the defendant signed the visitors book, which, if the committee had not found out he was working, would have entitled him to a furtber SUM of sick pay in addition to that he had already received. Thomas Rigby, brickgetter foe Messrs. Crosfield's, residing at 35, Helsby-street, sail he wa one of the committee of the eick club. In consequence of hearing that the defendant was working at Winwick Asylum witness was appoicted to visit him. On the Bth March he went to the defendant's house at 6.55 p.m. The rules stated that no sick person must be out after 7 o'clock. He stayed in the street about five minutes when he saw defendant coming in. Witness asked him if he had obtained the certificate he promised, and he replied that the doctor said be was still unfit for work, but he had not given him a certificate. He then signed the visitors book with the object of continuing on the sick list. His general conduct was that he wasn't work- ing, and that he was still unable to work. William Ernest Clegg, secretary of the sick club, 45, Marburc-street, produced the rnles of the club. John Downey, timekeeper at Winwick Asylum, of 57, Horsemarket-street, said the defendant began work at. Winwick Asylum on the 27th February as a joiner's labourer. He had been continually at work since. Superintendent Monaghan said he was present at the Court that morning, and during the hearing of the case heard the defendent say that be did obtain the 9s 8d by false pretences. The defendant said he had no evidence to offer. The Chairman : I clammy you have a strong feeling in the matter and we have no right to inquire into it. But 1 must say you are throwing away all possible chance of keeping your chairacter something like straight amongst your fellows. The Defendant: 1 cannot see how they can stop the money. The Chairman: That is no defence. If you have a grievance your acceptance of the offer made will not prejudice your right to have it heard. It doesn't help you by having it erewsierred tn the Quarter Sessions. Councillor Smethurst said bYendorsed the remarks of the chairman. If the defendant had senteone to advise him he was sure he would accept the offer made. The Defendant still refused ti accept the offer and the magistrates committed him to the Quarter S,esiions at Liverpool, and allowed him hail, himself in one surety of £lO and two others of 120 each. As he was leaving the dock, some of his fellow, work- men, who were in court, gave him the money to pay, as Mr. Browne suggested. Defendant retur •ed to the dock,and threw the money on to the solicitor's desk, smiling. The Chairman said such conduct did his felloe -work- men infinite credit, and he asked Mr. Browne whether he would allow the money to be paid. Mr. Brownie said he thought the case should go to Liverpool now. Defendant was simply laughing at them, and he did not say a word about being sorry for it. After further private consultation between Mr. Browne and a number of persons connected with the Sick Club. Mr. Browne said delendant still showed no signs of sorrow. The case would have to go to Liver pool.

There was one thing which certainly increased War- rington's chances of SUCCASS, and that was the state of the weather. For two hours preceding the encounter there was a miniature blizzard,which carpeted the coun- try, and the ground included, with a good two inches of snow. I never saw Watersheddings undersuch peculiar conditions. Snowshecidings would have been a better name on this occasion. Posters on the walls announced that tee match would certainly be played as the ground was protected. Protected against what? Net against snow evidently for there was plenty of it on the ground. The sight from the press-box was one to be remembered. Spectators were scarcer than ever they were before at an Oldham cap-tie. Instead of the serried ranks of enthusiasts usually to be seen at Oldham there was a thin line down two Bides, and a few lonely in- dividuals under the stands. £2B was the total of the receip s. Heavens I What a sell. Poor Warrington expected anything from £5O to £lOO as the outcome of this match. It has been their one sweet dteana for months to share that half-gate at Oldham, and now whee it has corne,Warrington's share,after expenses are paid, will be something under £lO. Could anything be more ageravating ?—and to lose in the bargain. iii, welt! there's a good time corning—so the song says.

It was natural that there should be some aelay in commencing the game. Footballers are not easily daunted, but the prospect of a cup-tie in twe inches Js: snow, with more falling, was certainly not alluring. The Warrington men were wise however,and,recognising that the state of the ground would suit them better than the speedier Oldhainites, they stuck out for playing and the game commenced nine minutes after time. Before the kick off the linesmen picksd up a flag-stick each and carefully drew a more or less straight line to represent touch." This operation was watched with amuse- ment by the spectators, who made many sarcastic com- ments on the drawing education of the linesman and their awful inability to see straight. Preseltly •-•1 men came round with brushes and made a commodious channel for a touch line.

Warrington's policy was soon apparent. They went in for short punts and quick following up, and very soon had their clever opponents in difficulties. Just about holding their own in the packs, and giving Bate and Asburst poSsession with frequency,the forwards did good service and were ably supported from behind. Up to the interval Warrington were the best team and had hard luck in not scoring. Oldham seemed unable to reconcile themselves to the strange conditions, and when half-time come with the scoring board blank, there was plenty of reason for hopiikg that Warrington would manage at least to make a draw of it. But somebody must have given Oldham a talking to. On changing ends they went about their work very differently. The fn. wards scrummaged fiercely and the backs resorted to their own particular nassing game. For a bit the machinery worked st:ffie, bat at last it got going and Tom Davies raced in at the corner after a finished movement. Soon after this Martin followed suit at the opposite side, though there is no doubt that Lees knocked on badly in passing to the scorer. The try was allowed however, b4th goal kicks failing. War- rington were beaten fore and aft now, and Joe Lawtoa and Tom Davies each got in again before the close, S. Les kicking a goal. Though beaten, Warrington were not:smothered by any means and gave Oldham one or two anxious moments. In the first minute of the second half Hockenhull gotacrosi the line and had very hard luck in beir.g robbed of a try.

Little can be said of the players individually. On the Warrington side every man did well,Hallam in particular deserving praise for the amount of work he got through in good style. His vis a vis, Thomas, performed finely also and was a continual stumbling block to Warrington. Fletcher and Davies were tke pick of the threequarters, the former putting in some vary powerful play in the centre. I like his style, and though he Is not so clever as Davies. Torn Fletcher is a threequarter any team would be glad of. For sound fo)tbill and hard work he will take a lot of beating.

In the Northern Union Cup.ties played in Lancashire on Saturday, three Yorkshire clubs figured, and of these only one survived—Hmulet. And they were lucky to win. Their match took puce at hwinton, and, after a most disagreeable game, they claimed victory by a „,_ .1 (two points) to nothing. Messer was ordered off by toe referee, and Pearson was off the field with an injury tor about a quarter of an haur. Tile crowd C.—) wed their disagreement with the decisions of the referee by waiting for him after the match and hooting him. A number of police were requisitioned. Badger played for Swinton, but got few chances. The Swinton people will probably regret having shown their displeasure with the referee in such brcible fashion, for their little sod- throwing practice was punished at the Northern Union meeting on Monday evening by the suspension of the ground until the cud of next October. Thus, for vasons have tbe Swinton f.)ll‘ been deprived of home matches for the first part of tha season. They will learn sense in time no doubt.

Runcorn were very unfortunate at Leigh, wher'. a fly- kick from the ground sent the hall over the bit an gave the homosters vict )ry. Leigh's rink has ben an impor- tant factor in their succ,ss this season. Widnes and Sal- ford had both easy journeys into the third round, their opponents b3ing Do wshury and Wereeth respectively. At Broughton it was a cas3 of Weic,ht &gal= weight and a little science, the Bangers claiming the latter which stood them in good stead. Toey managed to defeat the Hull Kingston Rnvers rather eastly by ten poiateto five. The Ravers were very much let down in the back

OUR LOCAL CITURCIIES.

"WHY DID JESUS DIE ?"

Tnig was the subject of a discourse by the Rey. John Yonge in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Si:lmlay evening. Ho based his remarks on the text "For Gild so [creel the world that He gave His only begotten son, that whosover helieveth in Him ahnuld not perish bet have everlasting life." John 3-16 v. He said that dur- ing the coming week the foremost place in the thought, possibly of millions of the humaa race would be the crucifixion of Jesus. That sunreme event would receive commemoration in all parts of the world. And yet he was afraid some of them had not clearly approbendsd the ultimate truth and ultimate purpose of that death. It was impossible to realise why Jetris died and then re- main unmovei spectators of the cross. If the question " Why did Jesus die ?" were asked, a variety nai reasons would be given. A few might be found willing to say He dial in attestation of the truth of His tsaching ; that He died as the suprsmest martyr to win testimony for God and truth; others would probably say that Jesus died out of love for the world; to reveal and ad- minister the infinite love of God ; to win nrn by bound- less compassion, pity, love, and infinite self sacrifice. But that was not the whole reason-, Jesus himself =est specifically declared that the reason of His death was to prevent a spiritual irremediable catastrophe to every living soul. In the words of the text he diethat who- soever believeth in Him should not perish. Then did it not follow that whosoever believeth not in Him should perish ? " Perish," whatever that might be, was the destiny of the.se who were not believers in Christ. In an earnest and touching manner the preacher proceeded to expound the belief that Chr'st was bearing our sins in His own body on the tree, and appealed to his hearers to believe in and accept Christ as their Siviour. At the Mose, an effective rendering of the anthem "There is a green hill far away " was given by the choir,Mrs. R. H. Pearson efficiently taking the solo part.

WYCLIFFE- P.S.A. SOCIETY.

Last Sunday was prize day in connection with this society when 413 book prizes were distributed to mem- bers whe had participated in the prize scheme. The chair was occupied by Mr. A. Lang, who spoke of the interest he took in the various branches of worlc connoc• ted with the society. One of the advantages of the society was that it had provided the members with good vr,holesome literature, which otherwise they would pro- bably not be in Dossessien of. Reference was also made to the good work being done in connection with the sick fund and the benevolent fued.—Mr. Ness,of Manehester delivered the address, and spoke upon "Heredity and Responsibility." He stated that some people sought to make the subject of heredity the scapegoat for their backsliding, but he pointed out that there wag in every man a God-given power by which he could resint tempta- tion. A nun's ancestors might determine for him what should be the nature of his trial but the iss le of that trial rested with himself. Ho vii-hed young men to realise that when they were trying to throw the respon- sibility on the shoulders of another they were playing the part of a moral coward. He wanted them to cultivate and develop that which was good, and seek the assist- ance of Christ.—The meeting was also taken part in Isv the Rev. John Yonge, Mr. F. Brown. and Mr. Whetnall.

The social con- science could never be strong and healthy while indivi- dual consciences wereweak and sicklq. BOROUGII OF WARRINGTON WARRINGTON CORPORATION BILL, POLL OF OWNERS AND RATEPAYERS. WHEREAS A PUBLIC MEIITING of the Owners and Ratepayers of the Borough of Warrington was duly held on Wedneaday,the 18th day of January. 899, in the ciymnasium Palmyra-equare. in the said Borough, in pursuance of the Act of the 35th and 39th Victoria, cap. 91. intituled " an Azt Ito authorize the Application of Funds of Municipal Corpora- tions and other governing bodies in certain cases." AND WHERHAS at the sail Meeting held at the time and place aforesald. I, as Chairman of such Meeting. and in com- pliance with the Statute proposed the following Resolution— That this Meeting, as representing the Owners and Rate- payers of the Borough of Warrington. ao, pursuant to the provisions of the ;act of the 35th and 35th Victoria, chapter 91, intituled " An Act to authorizs the Applica- tion of hinds of Municipal Corporations and ether governing bodies in certain cases," approve of and con- sent to the following resolations of the Council of the said anatugh, passed at their Meeting held in the Town Hall, Warrington, on the 3rd day of January, 1899 namely That the Town Couneil do prcmote a Bill in the next Session of Parliament for the construction of additional 'Waterworks and the Improvement of the Borough 'of Warrington, and for other purposes." "That the costs and expenses in relation to promoting " such Bill be charged upon the Borough Rate or other " Rates and Funds under the control of the Council as " the Council may decide." AND WHEREAS the said resolution, on being put, was carried, whereupon demand was made that such question should be decided by a Poll of owners and Ratepayers. AND WHEREAS such Poll of the Owners and Ratepayers of the Borough of Warrington has been duly taken by melts manner by law required under the provls ons of the Public Health Act, 1875, by mean. of Voting Papers. Majority in favour

AND I HEREBY FURTHER GIVE NOTICE, certify and declare that a 3 tha result of tfui said Poll the said refolution has been duly passed and adopted. Dated the Bth day of March, 1899. JOHi EDWARD WRIGHT, Mayor.

MARCH 30th 1899.

SEVERAL persons were arrested Brawling and Balancing, or will be summoned for brawl- ing in connection with very High Church services at Liverpool on Sunday. The law on this subject is plainly in favour of the Clergy. It does not permi tof even fespectful pro- tests by the laity against practices which may be illegal. The remedy in accordance with the law is a long and troublesome one; and the Bishops need not take any notice of the complaints made to them, if they think fit to disregard them. This lact is well known, and it helps to account for the growth of Ritualism. There has been no really Popular check, and the Bishops have been indis- Posed to act. The clergy have been encouraged bY this state of things to be indifferent to the feel- . lugs of many members of their congregations, who bave given up thGir protest in despair. The re- vival of the Protestant spirit has now stirred the laity to make open protests and to run the risk of 'being convicted for brawling. Obviously, this is a raost unsatisfactory state of things; but it pro- tects Divine worship from interference and dis- turbance, and, to this extent, it is a good thing all round, for Churchmen as well as for Noncon- lormists. It appears as if, in Lancashire and elsewhere, the question of brawling might assume new aspect. Protests due to difference of opinion • 'on religious matters ought clearly to be prevented Whilst public worship is going on. Nonconform- ists have always an easy remedy in their church tleetings and in the withdrawal of pew-rents, Weekly offerings, and subscriptions. Churchmen have no such remedy. They are never brought lace to face with the clergymen under conditions Which give laymen authority to speak and compel the clergymen to listen. They must put the ma- 'ellinery of law into force, and, as we have said, the ihettoP can stifle the voice of indignation and c°lllplaint. The clergy, on the other hand, claim to be rather outside the law. They can Punish a layman for brawling, but they l'einain unpunished for the illegality against which the layman may be pro- testing. The Bishops seem just as ready to pro- tect the clergy againt the laity as to protect the m again4t the law. They balance matters in a casu- istical way, and do very little. They are just now engaged in a series of balancings. They have ap- 13einted a Dean of Arches, who is a High Church- flan, without consulting the Government. They are about to sit in some kind of ecclesiastical court DY their Archbishops, to settle questions of ritual, the hope that the Clergy will accept decisions inich have not the force of law, and are not legallY binding upon anybody. The clergy en- °ree the law of the land against the laity, whilst their utmost to evade the law of the land on 4ieeir own account. We have called the latter attitude balancing, with a desire to refrain from living it a harsher name. Many persons will use 4 Illuch stronger term, and call it a conspir- teY• It remains to be seen how far the stronger ~,erna is justifiable. The law requires that the lie.urt it has set up shall be rasp scted. Are the 11311013 s setting up a court of their own outside el" above the law? It looks like it. If it be so, e ere will be a more acute crisis than the one which i.48t3 at the present moment, shaking the Estab- 41811reent to its very centre.

WHETHER we like it or 11"*t, Devolution. devolution grows. The ap- pointment of Royal Commis- 13iOn -3 racans that Parliament has not time for in- Which used to be undertaken by com- or relieving ftnittees. The Grand Committee is an instrument the House of Commons of minute We'tk, Which it seems to relish less and less. Bills ate sent to Grand Committees, in some cases be- 211se they are imperfectly and hastily drawn, and "en clauses have to be accommodated to the best

opinions attainable. The Government, which uses the Grand Committee to relieve the House,declines to send the question of private members' rights to an ordinary committee of inquiry, preferring to treat the said member as if he were a nuisance. It is curious, and it may be denied, that with every step in the work of devolution the House of Commons gets more limp and boneless. It does not want to do serious work of any kind. Members attend, they are ready to vote, but they do not care to listen to anything which ' makes any demand on their minds. They hand over to the Government whatever the Govern- merit wants, in an abject, docile, and subservient spirit that is sometimes painful to watch. As for co-operation, intelligent discussion, determined in- spiration, there is very little. There has been a de- volution of a most remarkable kind. It is, indeed, almost a farce to speak of legislation when so many members are mere counters in the political game. The efficiency of Parliament ought to be promoted by devolution, but the effect of it in the present Parliament has been to deaden it. Members of the majority are ready to give up anything but ease. They will not attend in any number for the daily business that goes on in the House. They prefer to lie invisible, and in the library, the reading- room, and the smoking-room. The Members' lobby once crowded with members is now often a desert. ' What is the use of having any opinions and talk- ing about them? The two parties to the coalition have surrendered their opinions. They devolve their views upon the Ministry ; and there are times when the Ministry is eager to perform a like feat. Responsibility is shifted at one time to the Bishops, at another time to the railway companies. The great devolution desired some years ago was scouted as a demoralising idea. Well, we have got devolution in other forms, and it is producing a decline in individual convictions, lessened interest in public affairs, patchwork legis- lation, wire-pulling for statesmanship, and a general desire to get rid of real responsibility. Some persons are unable to see these changes, be- cause they have caught the prevailing unwilling- ness to be logical and serions. But it cannot last much longer, and when the country has made up its mind to effect a change we shall return to days of more vigour and members of mere grit.

It must be a matter for satisfaction that wealthy men like Mr. Longstaft are prepared to d 3 for ex- ploration what the Government does not deem it right to undertake as the guardians of the National purse. The gift of .C 25,000 from Mr. Longstaff for the British Antarctic Expedition will go a long way for the furtherance of the scheme. It has been urged against the Premier's decision not to apply the taxpayers' money for this object that this country would have fared badly had not Englishmen been pioneers of civilisation; but at present we have abundance of territory,and science has pretty clearly demonstrated that, however interesting the discoveries at the Antarc- tic Pole, they cannot be very profitable. On the other hand, any moßey that we can fairly draw from the taxpayers can be profitably spent in improving the condition of the poor. The man Robertson, who was hanged this week in Newgate, was an example of the type produced by low living in London. Killing an innocent child from some petty motive of jealousy against the parent, he was indifferent to his fate, and went to his doom with a callousness that might easily be mistaken for bravery. The Old- Age Pensions Commission, as was pointed out in the Commons debate last week, elicited that one out of every three persons reaching sixty-five in London is in receipt of poor relief. While that is so the State cannot tax the people for vague tours of exploration. For, with our present system of indirect as well as direct taxation, the poorer people have to bear a considerable share of the burden of State.

There has just been a new development in the petroleum flesh-point question. It will be remem- bered that the Government threw out Mr. Reckitt's Bill for raising the flash point to one hundred degrees, and the opinion was freely ex- pressed by Government supporters that the oil was all right, and that it was the lamps which wanted attending to. How will they reconcile this idea with Mr. Jesse Collings new Bill, which proposes to raise the flash-point from 73 degress to 85 degrees ?. If the flash point requires to be raised at all then it should be raised to the standard of safety advocated by the wit- nesses before the Select Committee and the lead- ing experts of the country—and that is 100 de- grees. It is no use whatever adopting any half measure such as Mr. Jesse Collings proposes, and which he probably thinks will help to appease everybody. Oil with a flash-point of from 80 to 85 degrees .has undoubtedly been the cause of many fatalities and accidents of a terrible nature, and it will not mend matters in the least to raise the flash-point by these few degrees. We hope the Government will have the common-sense to see this and not make itself still more ridiculous by adopting the new Bill emanating from Mr. Jesse Collings.

The discomforts experienced in Parr Hall are again coming to the front. Since the Musical Society's concert on Wednesday last week com- plaints have been load, deep, and general with re- gard to the condition of things which obtained on that occasion. Before entering the building, pat- rons of the concert had to tramp through two or three inches of snow, which no one seems to have thought fit to remove from the footpath. This was by no means a trifling annoyance to people in evening dress, and especially to the lightly 1-iod ladies. Once inside the Hall and the immediate necessity for the retention of overcoats and wraps was apparent, and it is but slight exaggeration to say that there was a distinct resemblance be- tween the occupants of the front seats and Nansen's party of "Farthest North" explorers. And this at a concert which is admitted to be second to none given in Warrington! But there was worse to come after the concert was over. On venturing outside a totally incomprehensible scar- city of police was discovered, the result being that many ladies and gentlemen had to race about in the snow seeking their carriages themselves. We do not wonder that Mr. F. Monks and Mr. W. E. Atkin (the hon. secretary of the Musical Society) have been led to express, through the medium of our columns, their opinion of the management of Parr Hall in strong terms. It is amazing that there should be ground for such grave complaints. Sorely the Corporation is capable of attending to the comfort andconvenience of the people v;ho hire the Hall and attend the entertainments provided therein. We have a difficulty in finding any excuse whatever for the terrible state of matters on this particular evening, but we have a suggestion to make to the committee managing Parr Hall. If they can- not undertake to warm the interior of the always more or less cheerless building, let them close it for public purposes, and call it " The Corporation of Warrington's Cold-air Stores."

The Board of Management of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary is to be congratulated upon the satisfactory report they were able to lay before the governors of the institution at the an- nual meeting on Thursday afternoon. An in- creased income and a slightly diminished expendi- ture have enabled the Board te /educe the deficit of £1,130 4s. 6d. by £434 4s. - 9d. Two other such successful years would be sufficient to entirely wipe out the outstanding deficit of £7OO. Last year's success must therefore not be made the grounds for a smaller subscription or a diminution of aid in any form whatever, but on the contrary should serve as a stimulus to greater efforts in order to place the institution on a firmer financial basis. It is gratifying to notice that the workingmen of Warrington support the Infirmary right loyally. Last year's subscriptions from the
