RUNCORN CHARACTER SKETCHES.

BY " Q E.F.D."

What a curious chap he is to be sure! It is , questionable if you will ever see his equal should you live to be as old as Methuselah. A thorough- bred Bohemian in his ways, yet at the same time— most wondrous combination—a veritable tient Pry. There you see hira at all hours; in early morn be- fore the lark has commenced her roundelay, and also at night when nought but the sounds which emanate from the feline race are audible. Peeping round odd corners with an inquisitiveness which would do credit to one of the female sex; scouring round side streets keeping himself singularly aloof from every one. A strange character indeed ; how he subsists and manages to keep up appearances is inexplicable. Nothing is known of him save that he has been in the town for a considerable length of time. Whence he cometh or whither he goeth is an inscrutable mystery. We should not like to say as the ephemeral novel- ist might, that he is a 'bold bad man." If his face is to be trusted and physiognocnists avow that character is indelibly stamped on the figure head of every one, we should assert that the " meek, mild aspect" which he possesses is iniicative of that kind benevolent disposition which is found interwoven in the moral traits of the true philan- thropist. It is not necessary to make a word picture by describing him. It is sufficient to say that of all the persons who occupy prominent posi- tions none are so well known to all classes as the person who occupies present attention. It cannot be said that he associates with the rich any more -than with the poor. He is equally at home with both blesses whea he enters into their company. He is despised bY-Aenetetsection of the community; and , commended by another, while the third is t3tally indifferent to his good or bad traits. But what need is there to descant on his virtues or vices? Does not, the immortal bard say that there is good in everythine ? Has not every person though steeped to the eyes in vice one re- deeming virtue? Again is there any man so phar- asaical as to claim perfection? Is there not some dead fly in the golden pot of ointment which the sinless one may claim to posses? The soul who is striving to tread down the riiing passions of doubt and despair and attaia the perfect know- ledge of truth alone knoweth the answer. And so it will be sufficient for the reader to know that he is a mixture of the good bad and indifferent char- acteristics which are the make up of the average in- dividual. He his been known to be generous when it cost him nothing, and make sacrifices when it was of ultimate benefit to him. He quotes moral precepts to those who know him best—and they are few—but never embo lies them in his life. He is fond of a joke when someone else is the victim- ised party. En ,short he is a compound of extremes and a combination of all that is peculiar. He has no eye for the beautiful " A primrose by the rivers brim. A yellow primrose is to him And it is nothing more," and yet be sees in some things—noticeably the gol- den doubloons—a symmetry of form. He dislikes work, and yet has been known to work cheerfully. He never seeks for a good word from those who speak his praise and continually seeks to do good to niraself. Perlieps the town lies benefited, and is still benefiting tnrough his instrumentality. If it be true that it is an ill wind that blows nobody SOM3 good, then someone must have reaped consi- derable advantage, for when he is on business bent there are generally 'several ill winds in succe-sion. It must not be thought for a moment that he stands as a type of any section of the community. He is an oddity. He is brought into prominence because of his peculiarities. Not because he repre- sents even a small number of his fellows. It would be an easy task to fill two or three columns were ,a thorough delineation of his character deemed ad- visable. Two things, however, may be noticed for they occupy prominent positions in his peculiar " make up." They are, fist, his intense love for notoriety ; and secondly, a morbid craving for all that is sensational. If it were possible he would always be before the bar of public opinion; whether it were for censure or approbation is of little censepence so long as his ambition was realized. His pas tioe for diving into things startling—minding everybody's business but his own—is hardly to be commended. It has a tendency, we think, to debase and degr ,de, rather than elevate and ennoble, the intellect. He is sadly devoid of the finer senses but makes up this defici- ency by a ruggedness which may bespeak the diamond in the rough. This, of course. being a statement upon which the reader is called t 3 exer- cise his critical faculties.

EPIGRAM. A mart it would be hart to beat, For Machiavellian traits ; Virtue in union with deceit His daily life displays. A foe to all a frie ut to none ; His onward path he treads alone.

NEWTON CRICKET AND BOWLING CLIJR„

RUNCORN NOTES,

LBY A CORRESPONDENT

The Runcorn Philharmonic Society's concert— the last of the season—is now an event of the past. Yet, there will long live in the memory of those who attended it, recollections of a musical treat which has not been surpassed in Runcorn. The Saciety attempted a great deal in attacking Gounod's " Mersa Solennelle," but this audacity— if I might use the expression—has been fully justi- fied by results.

Several of the Guardians of the Runcorn Union went out of their way t ) cry shame on certain actiorts of a loci! lodge of Oddfellows. To hear the ring of indignation in the voice of one member when he said "it pauperises the man," was mag- nificent, and so was the ge;ticulation that accom- panNd it. Yet it was rubbish ! " What's in a name ?" You confine a man in a workhouse; you persecute his poor relations, and draw flora them c3atributions to his maintenance. 'Tis grand! They are not "paupers." Why Slmething is paid. Verily, verily," what's in a name?" There might be Sol}l3 distinction in .the pronunciation of the word pauper, but there is none in the treatment of the—well, "pauper."

The Friendly Society is perfectly justifiel in passing such a rule; beside q tney sboff great fore- thought. The object of all Friendly Societies,' take it, is t) contribute to the welfare of its members. Now, I contend, if a member comes to so sorry a strait as to be compelled to seek Parochial aid and shelter, and is in due course removed to a Work- house; he receives not an iota of benefit if the Guar- dians are able to bleed either his" club" or his" re- lations." Tae indignant Guardians may depend upon it, if they will give the inmate, who belongs to the Frienily Society the full benefit of a just reward of thrift the" offensive and hard" rides will soon be deleted.

The contest for seats on the Urban C3uncil has now been decided. As I said some weeks agJ the only fight of much interest was that between Mr. Handley and Mr. Shaw, junior, for _Bridgewater Ward. The figures were 185 to 114, and consider- ing the smallness of the poll Mr. Snaw is to be con- gratulated oa making a capital fight against bug odds. Mr. Han Hey, as is well known, is an old and well tried servant of the town, and even he must admit that he was fought honourably and run close.

The estimate, upon which the School Board rate is founded, has been just laid before a special meet- ing of that body—which, it must be borne in mind, is now constituted of gentlemen mostly pledged to " keep down the rate !" It discloses nothing Bur prising to those well versed in the educational ques- tion, though certain sections of ratepayers will be alifected Sy just a slight increase, Toe precept issued on the overseers is for £l.OOO for the half year, to be called for in eums:of £5OO. This com- pare] with the corresp3nding half year is, I under- stand a slight increase of £l5O. Alas! another fallacy has been exploded !

it is pleasing to be able to congratulate one's self occasionally ; and one likes to see the fruits of one's remarks. Therefore it is pleasing to note that a remark of mine referring to the wanton destruc- tion of property in Runcorn has been productive of vcid.

The case at the Police Court was a step in the right direction.

A CALLOUS .FATHER.

At the Warrington Blrough Pol,ce Court on Monday before the Mayor (Alderman J. E. Wright), Dresidinr;, and other magistrates, Edward Mee, M3rris -Court wes charged with making use of obscene language in Morris- court on the 18th March and with wilfully exposing his children, Walter and Ellen —Defendant pleaded euill'l7. —P C. Muakett said at 11-30 p.m. on Friday the 17th March be was called upon by defendant's wife to go to Morris Court. It was a very cold night and when he knocked at the door defendant put his head out of the bedroom window and used filthy language to him. Witness tried to persuade him to let his wife and children in but he would not. He called again at 12.15 with the same result, and at one o'clock the wife and children were standing in the street just the same. The youngest child was 8 months old and had nothing but a petticoat on. The other child was Byears of age,and bad neither carejacket nor muffisr ost. They were exposed from 10 30 until one o'clock, when they went into a neighbour's helm:, in Eldon-street.—Ellen Mee. wife of defendant. said her husband went to bed and left her and her children in the kitchen. He afterwards came cliwnstairs being drunk, and asked them what they were whispering abut. He ran at witness to hit her and when her daughter said he should not do that he ran at her. Witness unlocked the door when he got her by the throat and throttled her. He said he would knock all their heads off. The children ran out of the house because they were afraid of him. She repeatedly asked her husband to let them in. That had happened before about two months ago. She had only been married about twelve months to him and the oldest children were by a former wife —Defendant ex- pressed his sorrow for what had occurred and said it would net happen again.—The Chairman said he de- served ;twee months for his conduct. They were dis- posed to be lenient but he woad be severely dealt with if it occurred again. He was fined ss. for the obscene language and 103. and costs or 14 day for the second Olarge.

RAILWAY ALTER ‘TIONS AT WAItitINGTON.

Some very impertene alteradoas are in coarse of construction on the london and North-Western Railway at Arpley, for the par ticulers of whict, we are indebte I to the courtesy of eir.Gates,of the firm of Messn. Gates and •lholias, contractors for he workeind Mr. elogg, one of the engineers in charge. As a matter of fa tt the work has been in progress for about five mouths and consequently in a fairly .advanced state. The contractors are constructing marehelling sidings in Arpley Meadows between the grand junc- tion line and the Waltoa Junction line,and are con- necting the grand janction litre hige level line by a new road line to the low level marshalling sidings. The marshaLing sidings will cross the existing roadways, and it is the purpose of the b. & N. W., Reilwey Company to put 11 13 GV roads in their place which will cross over the Walton junction t 3 Arpley and Bank quay railway lines by twe bridges which will come out in Wilson- Patten-street. The district where the work is pro- ceeding is now almost unrecognisable, the aspect of the place being entirely changed by the navvies employed who number about 150. A temporary road has been laid down alongside what is to be the future road, and though it is only a makeshift it is so satisfactory that a cyclist can traverse it without saying anything of an unparliameutary nature, and r considered as a detail, its condition is most credit- able to Messrs. Gates and Thomas. The material or "filling"—to give it its proper name—used ia the building of the future road is brought from the Ship Canal at Acton Grange by the contractor's steam engine "Siriar," which, though not a large engine draws a tremendous amount,being loaded at Acton Grange by a steam- navvy, and-emptying its waggons at Arpley. The marshalling sidings—for the heavy traffic which has been rapidly growing has finally necessitated these alterations—will be composed of six lines of rails and the sidings will be about 400 yards long. The •contractors are well aware that sometimes, and not infrequently, the meadows in the Arpley district have a habit of flooding in rainy seasons and at high tides,and as a preventive have laid rows of 18in. pipes which will run right through the sidings to carry off the water. The roadway when completed will be of an easy gradient, rising slowly until the bridges are reached close to Wilson Pattern street, from thence the road will descend in an easy gradient as before into the street name I,but in order not to have the gradient too abrupt, the distance not being far from the bridges to the street, the road will make a detour. The bridges are in an advancel state. The brick work is practically completed, ready far the girder beds. Naturally, the construc- tion of the new road will obthte the necessity for the level crossing at Slutcher'e-lane wtich will be a decided improvement in more senses than one, and altogether the change which will he effected in the general serreaniings strike one as biiag a decided improvement.

PENS AND PISTOLS.

In the wild West of America during the lawless times hirty or forty years ago, the editors of the local newa- papers wrote their editorials with a loaded revolver at elbow ; with one eye on the manuscript and the other on the door. They wanted their knives and shooting rons handy, for in those days it was the custom to settle differ3nces without troubling the courts. In an argument of that sort the decision went for the man " got the drop" on his antagonist. A fraction of a second count.; in critical moments. Upon the whole, t may b 3 said that the fellow who is best prepared either to fight or fly is apt to live longest ; especially in periods of war or general violence.

You see the principle. It dosen't call for explanation. It is merely a common-sense rule of conduot. It means, be on the look out for things that are bound to happen sooner or later, and not be caught unawares. This is a world of narrow margins. The biggest storms and gales are likely to be those that nobody ex- punts. Calamity, like the red Indian, has a fashion of biez:ng away WI us from behind a tree. If my frienl Mclntyre, of Aherdeen, had seen that bit of cog-p 1 on the pavement in Oxford-street one day last August, hs would not have stepped on it. As it was, he fell anl broke his right arm . Of course, the utmoit watchfulness and prevision wit not keep us clear of all evil. Thera will always remain an opening for doctors ant insurance c ampanies. Per- hapsit might los unluolev to b 3 too fortunate—which looks a contradictory thing to say. But no fear. We shall ever be tumbling into scrip's enough tg furnish an incoms to tho 33 w:10 live out of ou • quirrels. our acci- dents, and our sins.

WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE YOUNG PEOPLE'S SOCIETY.

This society brought the season's programme to a successful close on Monday evening. The first part of the proceedings consisted of vocal and instru- mental iteras, and a waxwork entertainment occu- pied the remainder of the evening. The Rev. J. Yonge presided over a fairly-large audience. Solos were well sung by Mrs. S. W. Jeffery,Miss Rersha,w and Mr. Arthur Griffiths. The last named gentle- man also sang in company with Mr. Goldthorpe in the duet "The Battle Eve." Violin solos were creditably given by Mr. Tom Wallington. Mr. W. H. Payton played the pianoforte accompaniments with skill and taste. Two recitations were also con- tributed by Miss Farness. The waxwork enter- tainment was a great success, the characters repre- sented being faithfully delineated. Mr. James Ball the "proprietor " of the show, and Mr. Joseph Pod- more, his assistant, described the various figures. A pleasant evening concluded shortly before ten o'clock.

-ICE TO CORRESPONDENIS. . Authors of lett-ers intended for publication must in alt cases send their names and aidresses to the Editor. PARR HALL DISCOMFORTS. Sir,—Many complaints have reached me of the in- tense cold and consyquent great discomfort experienced by those attending the concert in Parr Hall on Wednes- ay last. In one case at least the complaint takes the form of a reflection on the committee of the Musical Society. May I (in defeece of my committee) state that no efforts were spared by the promoters of the conwet to make the Parr Hall less wretchedly cold than it undoubtedly was. The impossibility of accomplishing this was unfortunately only too plain to audience and performers alike. At 3 p.m. on Wednesdey last tie Soloists and orchestra rehearsed, and f r two hours the thermometer on the platform stood at 46 degree; whilst a roaring fire in the stove only succeeded in rasing the temperature to 50 degrees by 6-45 p.m. Mr. Powys (the caretaker of the hall) assured me that a fire hai been going constantly all we-k and that he was doing all in his power to make the hall werrn.At least three members of my o""mmittee and myself used all our influence to effect this during the whole of the afternoon and even log. I feel sure our friends and supporters will acquit- US on a charge of carelessness in a matter so nearly affecting our inter, es and the coufort of all concerned; and whilst sincerely regretting the state of things com- plained of I venture to hope the Parr Hall Committee (with whom alone is the power), will speedily de all that is neeessary to put in order the heating app ;rata.; end obviate the tea, ful and terrible driughts that abeund. My attention was also drawn to the dangerone and most inconvenient stets of Pelmyra Square, and the Street at the entrance to the Parr Hall. It is not quite apparent why the elorperatien saw fit to leave the snow unmoved and in !heaps in this particu'ar spot wh let compelling the ratepayers to their dut.y of clearing the pavemen's in front of their own houses.—l am, yours very truly,

W. E. ATKIN, Hon, Sec. the Warrington Mtnical Society. 22, Bo!cl-qtreet. March 24th 1899.

POULTOS-V7ITFI-FEA.RNHEAD. ADOPTION OF

LIMITING AND WATCIIING ACT.

Sir,—When the motion proposing that the parish adopt the Lighting and Watching Act Was brolviht Ism ard after the Parish Council meeting on March 6:h I objected to and protestei against the proceedings as /ming quite irregular on the ground of informality in the notice of the meeting. I again object to and hereby 'record my further protest against any action in sequence to the so-called " Aej ,urned Parish Wean; " of Jtn. 16-1, ea the rnand ab aye mentioned. Those who were oresent will remember the reasons which I gave for eoing so and which I will repeat for the infrormatioh of those not present. On Januar,- 16 h a P al.:eh Meeting Was dilly called to consider the desiraLility of the adoption or otherwise of the Act above mention, d, when, af-er some discuasian it W 2 s decided that in consequence of many retep avers being unaware of the meeting, and consilerung that the time when the Anew,' Parish Meting would be held was so near. it wou'd he better to postpone the con- sideration of the subject until that meeting. This was not carried out, but instead the matter was brought up in the Schoolroom at the close of the Parish Council meeting on March 6th, at what was called an " Adjourned Pa-ish M"eting" of that of January 16th, but as the meeting of ..-inuary 16 h never was anjaurned, and as the subject Was not introduced at the Annual P aish Meeting held an hour latnr the lame evening, I maintain that the whole proceedings smnectea with the movement are up to the prevnt The minute recorded on January 16th, upon which the so called " Adjourned Parish Meeting" was held reads as follows : "That WA consider the adoption of the Lighting and Watching Act on t'se evening of the annual Parish Meeting so as to get a fully representa- tive meeting of the ratepayers." This I submit is a meaningless and an insensate minute as it does not de- ne by whom nor where the question shall be cnnsidsrad on the "evening " mentioned. Certainly the only scrap -of meaning (if any) that can be implied to it is that the question shonld be reintroduced at the annual parish meeting which as I have previously stated was not done land even had it been so reintroduced the minute as -drafted limited the meeting to the" consideration " as no definite proposal hal been given notice of for the adoption of the Act.—Yours truly, WILLIAM HOUGH ON: Paddington,March 24th, 1899.

ST. HELENS.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A PAINTER

On Monday afternoon. Mr. F.La Jones,deputy coroner, held an inquest at Et. FIPP.:aI Town Hall on the body of George Slater (35). of 10, Parr Milloottazea, St. Relens,a foreman painter,emploved by the Great Central Railway Company,who died sudienly on Saturday. Deceased fol- lowed his work up to Saturday afternoon,when he went home, and. without eomplainine of any illness, ate a good dinner of potato and meat pie. He went to bed, but shortly afterwards called for his wife, and corn- plained of a severe pain in the stomach. Re afterwards suffered from sickness, and exclaimed, "I am ening ; God bless you all." A few minutes later he suddenly exp'r-d. The circumstances were reported to the caunty coroner, who ordered a post-mortem examination, and this was TRIVIR on Sunday afternoon by Dr. Fred Knowles. Dr. Knowles depoled that he found the man's heart and other orctans had been unhea thy, and his hearty dinner on Saturday had brought (In failure of the heart's action. A verdict of" Death from natural causes" was returned.

The Union calls , for a Union purposes in the corresponding halt of 1893, were at the rate of fivepence half-penny in the £on ratable values. Papers read to the Guardians included calculations made to encourage procedare with the long intended erection of buildings needed for the better accommodation, treatmeat,and nursing of the Fick poor. They advised acquisition of a new Infirmarynconstrueted in accordance with modern requirements ; the land pur- chase, and an increased sursrug staff to ea practicel without the calls upon townships CXOE eiing, for all Union purposes, sixpence in the £on rateable values. The tendency to increase local indebted- ness in England and Wales had in some former papers explanatory of Union estimates, received adverse com- ment, hence it was fitting upon occasion of laree ad- dition to their own liability, seeing too that the Board might have to exercise still further borrewing powers, that comparison of our procedure with that generally prevailing should be drawn. During tire 20 years 1878-97 the Imperial deb; fell from 775 millioms t 611 millions sterling,a decrease of 134 millions. In the saw.; period, the loc sl debt more than doubled itself by an in- crease of 138 millions ; it now amounted to the enor- mous sum of 252 millions sterling. The local debt had increased 120 per cent, and annual local taxation lead increased 77 per cent, agaittet an increase in popu- lation of 23 per ceet. and in rateable value of property of 26 per cent, rateable value not keeping pace with the grewte of the rates. A. movement with which the Board declined to join,was being made by Boards of Guardians, and many corpor- ate bodies connected with Local Government in euopert of the extension of periods of repayment of Lean Debts te sixty years. The large deputations received by the Government had net so far succeeded in their efforts to induce approval of the policy they advocated. In the concluding year of the century, the ratepayers of the Warrington Union succeeded to the lands and buildings , forming, apart from the New Infirmary and its site, tee Union Workhouse,withaut any debt or charge outstand- ing thereon. They succeeded alto to their valuable Pad- gate properties, obtained at a cost approaching £20,000 subject only to two loans with an outstanding liability ot about £7,000. In July 1907, one of the two loans would expire. In April 1910, the second would expire. The whole of the loan debt inclusive of recent loans, would be covered by one rate of one shilling aad ten pence in tee £, on the preeent [Mien rateable value. NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTIONS.

DECLARATION OF THE POLL.

The polling for dee election of a District Councillor in the Newton cad Viaduct Wards respectively took place on Monday. For the Newton ward the candidates were Mr. John Simoson, the retiring candidate. and Dr. Latham, the nominee of the Lab iur party. In the Viaduct Ward, the candidates were, Mr. Nathaniel Sidieiey. and Mr. George F. Clarke. For the Viaciact Ward tnere were two polling stations, at the District Schoo!, No 1, being presided over by Mr James B Porter with Mr T Marindate as p)11 clerk ; No. 2 booth was pr vsided over by Mr A. Bowes, with Mr 11 116ughton, aa poll clerk. The Newton Ward polling station wls at St. Peter's Schools, over which Mr. W Gorse presidel, with Mr Singleton as poll e'erk. The polls opened at twelve o'clock, but the vicinity of the polls wore a very cruet aspect, there be- im:, very few people an aut beyond the candidates and tieir won's. The poling proceeded very quietly until one o'clocx, when there was a sight rush, and between that hour and two o'clock, matters continued brisk. Daring the afternoon there was not much business done, but after six o'clock voters came in very rapidly,and up t the time of closing, eight o'clock, a large number of o:es were rogisterei. Oa the whole. however, the poll was nor a heavy one. The counting of th 3 votes took place at the Town Hall, Earlestown, Mr. W. W. Shirley, Clerk to the Council, officiating as returning officer. In rl-H room bsiisthe candidates' .agents, there were Mr G. F. Clarke, Mr. M. Siddeley, Dr. Latham, Mr. J. Simpson. Mr. W. Collingwood, Mr. W. Beattv, Mr. R. Barton, Szo. Toe counting proceeded expeditiouoy, and shortly atter nine o'clock, Mr. Shirley announced to those in the room that the results were as follows :

FRODg-HAM.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—The last meeting of this Council was held on Friday evening in the Court room, Frodsham, Mr. Charles E. Linaker presiding. The chief business was the re-eppointmrnt of Assistant Overseer, Mr. Jones. The parish Clerk reported the receipt of a letter from the Railway Company statlne that the mat- ter of better train service from Frodsham would be dealt with when arranging for the spring changes, and that tourlsts bookings will be fixed from the first of May next. It was resolved that Mr. George Jones be re-appeinted Assistant Overseer, with a Salary at the rate of £65 per annum, with extra remuneration at the rate of £65 for each special sanitary rate levied. PAROCHIAL COMMITTER.—The final meeting of the Frodaharn Parish Council, acting as a Parochial Corn- mittoe, was held in the Court Room, Frodsham, en Friday evening, there being present Messrs Charles E Linaker (presiding), Earlam, Rothwell, Shore, Kydd, Barreve, Midge, Gleave, Rodgers, Wilkinson, Diggle and Farrington. The salary to be paid to the clerk for ser- vices rendered during the past year was considered, and ultimately it was resolved that Mr Ashton have the sum of £25, the amount voted fcr the previous year. A letter was read from Mr James Edwards stating that' although he had paid for water for some time past he had not used any from the town's supply, and that on and after the 24th June next he would be exempt from further rating. Some discussion ensued, when the C:erk was directed to inform Mr Edwards that the committee had no power or authority to exempt him from payment of the water rate levied from time ,to time. Mr Kydd skated that the trees planted in the main streets re- quired more attention—an ozcasional watering—and suegested that if the committee thought desirable it would be as well that the occupiers living directly opposite should take charge of the tress, watering them when considered needful, and it was resolved that such should be done. Mr. Earlatrt proposed that a vote of thanks be accorded to the chairman for services rendered during the past twelve month. This was seconded by Mr. Kyckl, and a similar vote was passed to the vice- chairman.

LATEST NE WS• NORTHERN UNION CUP COMPETITION. ST. HELENS v. MORECAMBE. This replayed tie came off at Morecambe yesterday and again multed in a draw, both teams scoring a goal and a try. FATALITY ON THE SHIP CANAL. About three o'clock yesterday morning a number of men were engaged loading a steamer at the coal wharf of Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., Acton Grange, near Warrington, when one of them, William Webster, who lived at Walton, was knocked down and carried into the bunkers of the vessel along with the coal. The men at once went to his rescue, but it was some time before the body could be recovered, and life was then found to be exlnct. TERRIBLE COLLIERY ACCIDENT. 18 MEN INJURED NEAR ILKESI'ON. A shoc'aing cage accident occurred at Shipley Cole leries near Ilkeston, yesterday morning. A double decked cage, with eighteen mEn,was descending the shaft when the engine went wrong, and the cage dashed against the bottom. All the occupants were injursd, some severely. Eight of the worst cases were removel to Ilkeston Hospital. The ascending cage wrecked the roof of the engine house. ADVANCE IN PITDDLERS' WAGES. Ueder the slidiee sale arraogemect for the North of England, the wages of puddlers in the manufactured iron and steel trades were advanced yesterday morning by 3d. and forge and mill men's wages by 2i per cent. THE SWISS HOTEL FIRE. !ARNE, WEDNESDAY. The upper part of the hotel Beau Rivage at Interlaken was completely horned, as a result of the outbreak there yesterday. It is doubtful whether it ca übe temporarily repaired for the summer seas-m. CARELESS HANDLING OF DYN/MITE. TWO MEN KILLED. PARIS, WEDNESDAY. A terrible eeplosioe occurred ii a dynamite store at Jebel Jelin!, in the vicinity of Tunis, yesterday. A num. bar ot workmen were engaged in fastening a box of dynamite, using ordinary nails instead of screws, when the contents suddenly exploded. An Italian and a Mal. tese were killed, and another man named Nicholas was serime,ly injured. LATEST LOCA.L WEATHER FORECAST. The folllwine forecast of the weather for the ensuing twenty-four hours in the north-west of England was issued at the Meteorological Office at three o'clock yes. terday afternoon : Westerly and north-westerly winds, unsettled ; rain at times. Colder. FATAL LEAP FROM WATERLOO BRIDGE. Shortly after half past ten yeiterdiy morning con- siderable excitement war; occasioned on Waterloo Bridge. London, by a man mounting the parapet, waving his arms frantically above his head, and then deliberately plunging into the river. In less than three minutes the man was hauled out of the river by the Thames river notice. He was quite dead however. The man was fairly well dressed,apparently about 40 years of age,and had the appearance of an old soldier.

WESTON, PARISH COUNCIL ELECTION. The polling for the ele7tiort of eight members to re• present the Weston Ward on the Parish Council took place on Monday at the Church &twols. The annual parish melting nominated eleven candidates for tkO eiqht vats, none of whom withdrew. The presiding officer was Mr. G. F. Ashton, who also acted as deputy returning officer, and Mr. W. Flrovzh officiated as poll clerk. Out of au electirate of 220, 163 recorded their votes, most of the polling being done in the evening when the workine: men had ceased work. The result was dclirei at 8 30, the following being the figures John Ball.. John Ernest Cecil Bankes 67 Daulel Basnett jarres Basnett WI liam Duutabin 65 George Harry Garner 6l Arthur George Guest William Edward Urine David Somerville James Tudor Alfred Maitland Wood 76 The elected members are the Rev. Caoon Wood (vicar), Messrs. J. Tudor, James Basaett, A. G. Guest, J. E. C. Bankes, W. Dunbabin, D. Bisnett, and G. H. Garner. This was the only contest for Parish Council honour e in the Roncorn Union, the other psrishes being content Iwith the voice of the Parish meeting.
